Preface

The rapid evolution of deep learning has opened transformative avenues in genome mapping, computation, and medical data analysis. This book, Deep Learning in Genome Mapping: Computation and Analysis, is designed to provide a comprehensive yet accessible exploration of how artificial intelligence and optimization techniques are revolutionizing genomic science and healthcare applications.

The chapters in this volume are carefully curated to cover both foundational and advanced perspectives. Beginning with computational strategies and optimization algorithms for genome mapping, readers are introduced to the role of deep learning in addressing the complexity of large-scale biological data. Topics such as multi-objective evolutionary algorithms and deep learning–driven medical data engineering illustrate how diverse approaches converge to enhance accuracy and knowledge discovery in genomic studies.

The text also bridges methodological insights with real-world applications. Techniques integrating neural networks, image processing, and edge detection are presented alongside applications in smart healthcare and medical imaging. The inclusion of chapters on noise reduction strategies, AI-based heart delineation in chest X-rays, and innovations in medical science highlights the book’s interdisciplinary scope—linking genomics, imaging, and machine learning for practical impact.

While the focus remains on genome mapping, the broader context of healthcare and medical data analysis is deliberately incorporated. This ensures that the book speaks to a wide audience, including computer scientists, biomedical engineers, healthcare researchers, and students eager to understand the intersection of artificial intelligence with life sciences.

The contributors to this volume are experts who bring clarity to complex topics, balancing theoretical foundations with practical implementations. It is our hope that this book not only deepens the reader’s understanding of deep learning in genome mapping but also inspires further research and innovation in this rapidly advancing field.

We extend our gratitude to the researchers, practitioners, and academicians whose contributions make this volume a valuable resource. May it serve as both a guide and a stimulus for advancing intelligent solutions in genomics and healthcare.

Despite its reputation, BLAST and similar heuristic-primarily based tools face demanding situations in managing state-of-the-art large genomic datasets generated via excessive- throughput sequencing technologies.

Today, deep learning (DL) has emerged as effective tools for solving complex organic issues, including genome mapping. DL technique new, particularly, has shown promise in managing huge-scale information and identifying problematic patterns that traditional strategies. Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) and Recurrent Neural Networks (RNNs) had been widely utilized in biological collection analysis (Zhou and Troyanskaya 2015b). The integration deep learning combine into genome mapping remains an a location modern research, with challenges in computation time and resource performance. In recent years, state-of-the-art has emerged as a effective device for managing complicated and massive-scale datasets. Integrating deep learning architectures with optimization algorithms, it is viable to noticeably decorate the computational performance modern day genome mapping. This chapter introduces a deep latest-based optimization set of rules designed to improve the velocity and accuracy trendy genome mapping procedures. The proposed algorithm combine the strengths contemporary deep neural networks in sample recognition and the efficiency latest optimization strategies to offer a more effective solution for genome mapping.

3 Proposed Framework

In this phase, we define the proposed framework for genome mapping the usage of a deep getting to know-primarily based optimization algorithm (Joshi et al. 2024). The framework combine the proposed approach aims to lessen computational overhead even as preserving excessive accuracy in genome mapping. The deep studying model plays a crucial role in recognizing patterns and systems inside the genomic statistics. Framework, we utilize a CNN (Zhu et al. 2023) because of its talent in shooting spatial hierarchies in records. The deep learning (Lee et al. 2021) includes the following layers:

  1. Takes the preprocessed genomic data as input.
  2. Extracts capabilities from the input sequences through multiple filters.
  3. Reduces the dimensionality of the function maps, enhancing computational efficiency.
  4. Aggregates the found out features and generates a final representation of the genome.
  5. Produces a hard and fast of predicted gene mappings or relevant genomic markers.

The CNN is trained on labeled genomic datasets, learning to identify key patterns and structural variations within the genome. The training procedure makes use of backpropagation and gradient descent algorithms to reduce prediction error.

3.1 Optimization Algorithm

To similarly decorate the overall performance of genome mapping, we combine an optimization algorithm (Singh et al. 2024) to pleasant-song the version parameters and enhance mapping accuracy. particularly, we combine a variation of the Differential Evolution (DE) algorithm, which is thought for its robustness in solving complicated optimization issues. The optimization system follows these steps: Populace Initialization: A population of candidate solutions (model parameters) is initialized. Mutation: New candidate solutions are generated by means of combining existing ones through mutation. Crossover: The generated answers are combined with the modern population to explore the hunt area. Selection: The exceptional candidates are selected based totally on their health, which in this situation is the genome mapping accuracy. The DE set of rules is iteratively applied to improve the mapping accuracy by way of optimizing the weights of the deep studying model. This hybrid technique of mixing deep gaining knowledge of with evolutionary optimization enables us to effectively look for most appropriate mappings in the huge genomic records.

3.2 Framework Workflow

The workflow of the proposed framework can be summarized within the following steps:

  1. Step 1: Raw genomic information is accrued and preprocessed to take away noise and extract key features.
  2. Step 2: The preprocessed data is fed into the deep learning model, where key genomic patterns are learned.
  3. Step 3: The DE is applied to fine-tune the model parameters, improving the accuracy of genome mapping.
  4. Step 4: The optimized model generates the very last genome map, highlighting gene locations and crucial genetic markers.

4 The Proposed Methodology: Graph Neural Networks in Deep Learning

Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) are a class of deep learning algorithms particularly designed to deal with statistics that can be represented as graphs. Gnome mapping, GNNs (Pati et al. 2023) can be hired to seize the relationships among genomic elements, which includes genes, mutations, and regulatory areas, which often show off graph-like systems. GNNs work by way of aggregating information from neighboring nodes in a graph to study node and graph-degree representations. Let G = (V, E) be a graph, wherein V is the set of nodes (representing genes or genomic regions) and E is the set of edges (representing relationships or interactions among the nodes) (Mao et al. 2023).

4.1 Mathematical Formulation of GNNs

At each layer of a GNN, the representation of a node v ∈ V is updated by aggregating information from its neighbors N(v). The update rule for the node representation at layer l + 1 is given by:

(1)
hv(l + 1) = σ(W(l)⋅AGG({hu(l)∣u∈N(v)∪{v}}))

where:

  • hv(l) is the feature vector of node v at layer l.
  • N(v) represents the set of neighbors of node v.
  • AGG is the aggregation function (e.g., mean, sum, or max) that aggregates information from the neighbors.
  • W(l) is a learnable weight matrix at layer l.
  • σ is a non-linear activation function (e.g., ReLU).

The final node representations after L layers, hv(L) , capture both the local structure of the graph and the features of the nodes. The proposed Algorithm 1 is explained step by step procedure.

Algorithm 1 Graph Neural Network for Genome Mapping Require: Graph G = (V, E) with node features H(0).

Require: Number of GNN layers L, weight matrices W(l) for each layer l, and activation function σ.

Ensure: Node embeddings H(L) for each node v ∈ V.

  • 1: Initialize node features H(0) for each layer l = 0 to L − 1 do each node v ∈ V

  • 2: Aggregate information from neighbors:

    (2)
    mv(l) = AGG({hu(l)∣u∈N(v)∪{v}})

  • 3: Update node feature:

    (3)
    hv(l + 1) = σ(W(l)⋅mv(l))

  • 4:
  • 5:
  • 6: return Final node embeddings H(L).

4.2 Graph Convolutional Networks (GCNs)

A specific form of GNNs is the GCN, where the aggregation step is based on the normalized adjacency matrix of the graph. The update rule for a GCN (Rentao et al. 2024) layer is given by:

(4)
$$H^{(l + 1)} = \sigma\left( {\overset{\sim}{D}}^{- 1/2}\overset{\sim}{A}{\overset{\sim}{D}}^{- 1/2}H^{(l)}W^{(l)} \right)$$

where:

  • H(l) is the matrix of node features at layer l, where each row corresponds to a node.
  • Ã = A + I is the adjacency matrix of the graph with added self-loops (i.e., an identity matrix I).
  • D̃ is the degree matrix of Ã.
  • W(l) is the learnable weight matrix at layer l.
  • σ is a non-linear activation function.

The proposedAlgorithm 2 is explained step by step procedure. The update rule for each layer of the GCN is as follows:

Algorithm 2 GCN Layer Update

Require: Adjacency matrix Ã, degree matrix D̃, node feature matrix H(l) , weight matrix W(l) , and activation function σ.

Ensure: Updated node feature matrix H(l+1).

  1. Compute normalized adjacency matrix:

    (5)
    $${\overset{\sim}{A}}_{\text{norm}} = {\overset{\sim}{D}}^{- 1/2}\overset{\sim}{A}{\overset{\sim}{D}}^{- 1/2}$$

  2. Update node features:

    (6)
    $$H^{(l + 1)} = \sigma\left( {\overset{\sim}{A}}_{\text{norm}}H^{(l)}W^{(l)} \right)$$

  3. return Updated node features H(l+1).

4.3 Training a GNN

The GNN is trained by minimizing a loss function based on the task at hand. A common loss function for node classification is the cross-entropy loss:

(7)
$$\mathcal{L} = - \sum\limits_{v \in V}\sum\limits_{c = 1}^{C}y_{v,c}\log{\hat{y}}_{v,c}$$

where:

  • yv,c is the true label (binary), indicating whether node v belongs to class c.
  • ŷv,c is the predicted probability that node v belongs to class c, typically obtained using a softmax function.
  • C is the total number of classes.

The training process for the GNN is summarized in the followingAlgorithm 3:

Algorithm 3 Training Graph Neural Network

Require: Training data (X, Y ), GNN model with parameters θ, learning rate η, and number of epochs T.

Ensure: Trained GNN model parameters θ.

  • 1: for each epoch t = 1 to T do
  • Forward pass: Compute node embeddings H(L) usingAlgorithm 1.

  • 2: Compute loss function:

    (8)
    $$\mathcal{L} = - \sum\limits_{v \in V}\sum\limits_{c = 1}^{C}y_{v,c}\log{\hat{y}}_{v,c}$$

  • 3: Backpropagate and update parameters:

    (9)
    $$\theta = \theta - \eta\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\theta}$$

  • 4:
  • 5: return Trained model parameters θ = 0.

GNNs are mainly nicely-applicable for genome mapping because genomic statistics can be represented as graphs, wherein nodes correspond to genes or genomic areas and edges constitute relationships including regulatory interactions, gene co-expression (Feng et al. 2024). The proposed framework can study wealthy, node-level representations that capture each the character properties of genes and their relationships to other genes inside the genome. This result in more accurate genome mapping and a better know-how of gene features, interactions, and mutations. We present the GNN-primarily based deep getting to know approach for genome mapping within the form of an set of rules. This set of rules outlines the method of the next section.

4.4 Graph Neural Network with DE Optimization for Genome Mapping

The proposedAlgorithm 4 integrates GNNs with DE optimization to enhance the performance of genome mapping responsibilities. GNNs are effective in chooses the complicated relationships between nodes in a graph shape, along with genes or proteins in biological networks. The architecture involves more than one layers in which node functions are iteratively updated through an aggregation mechanism that considers neighboring nodes, allowing the version to study wealthy representations of the underlying data. The proposed technique, DE is hired as the optimization method (Joshi et al. 2024) to adaptively look for choicest weight matrices that govern the variations of the node features. DE is in particular appropriate for this challenge due to its robustness in exploring the quest area and its capability to deal with non- differentiable and complicated fitness landscapes, which might be not unusual in deep gaining knowledge of programs. The set of rules begins with the aid of initializing a populace of candidate solutions, every representing a fixed of weights for the GNN. DE generates new candidate solutions, enhancing upon the preceding generations based on their health. The health of each candidate is determined by means of the performance of the GNN in terms of category accuracy, computed using a loss feature that quantifies the distinction among expected and actual labels for the nodes. The selection method ensures that best the high-quality-appearing candidates live to tell the tale to the following technology, allowing the set of rules to converge toward an most suitable set of weights. The set of rules’s effectiveness can be attributed to its capacity to version complex dependencies in graph-dependent records while employing a global optimization technique that is much less susceptible to nearby minima. As a result, this method holds promise for diverse programs in computational biology, specially in obligations along with gene prediction, protein-protein interplay prediction, and other regions in which information the difficult relationships in biological networks.

5 Experimental Setup

This section outlines the experimental setup used to evaluate the proposed GNN with DE optimization algorithm for genome mapping tasks.

5.1 Environment and Dataset

The experiments were conducted on a machine equipped with an Intel Core i7 processor and 16 GB of RAM. The software environment included Python 3.8, utilizing libraries such as PyTorch for implementing the GNN and DE algorithms. The experiments were run on a Linux-based operating system to ensure compatibility with the required libraries and packages. For the evaluation of the proposed method, we used publicly available genomic datasets, specifically: Each document belongs to one of seven classes. A large dataset comprising 19,717 nodes and 44,338 edges, where nodes represent articles related to diabetes, classified into three categories. Dataset related to diabetes, available for download at https://linqs.soe.ucsc.edu/data

5.2 Evaluation Metrics

To assess the performance of the proposed algorithm, we employed the following evaluation metrics (Zhou and Troyanskaya 2015):

  • Accuracy: Accuracy is the ratio of correctly predicted nodes to the total number of nodes. Mathematically, it is defined as:

    (13)
    $$\text{Accuracy} = \frac{TP + TN}{TP + TN + FP + FN}$$

  • where TP, TN, FP, and FN represent the number of true positives, true negatives, false positives, and false negatives, respectively 13.

  • F1 Score: The F1 Score is the harmonic mean of precision and recall, providing a balanced measure of the model’s performance. It is computed as:

    (14)
    $$\text{F1\ Score} = 2 \times \frac{\text{Precision}\text{⋅}\text{Re\ call}}{\text{Pr\ ecision} + \text{Re\ call}}$$

    Precision and recall are defined as:

    (15)
    $$\text{Pr\ ecision} = \frac{TP}{TP + FP},\text{Re\ call} = \frac{TP}{TP + FN}$$

    The F1 Score provides a single performance measure by combining precision and recall 14.

  • Cross-Entropy Loss: The cross-entropy loss function is commonly used in classification tasks to measure the divergence between the predicted and actual labels. It is defined as:

    (16)
    $$\mathcal{L}_{CE} = - \sum\limits_{v \in V}\sum\limits_{c = 1}^{C}y_{v,c}\log{\hat{y}}_{v,c}$$

    where yv,c is the true label and ŷv,c is the predicted probability for node v belongs to class c. This metric helps in minimizing the difference between predicted and actual class probabilities 16.

5.3 Implementation Details

The GNN structure applied within the experiments consisted of 3 layers, with the activation characteristic. The preliminary node capabilities have been derived from the datasets, and the output layer became configured to fit the range of lessons in each dataset. The Differential Evolution optimization changed into set with a population length of 100, a mutation aspect F = 0.25, and a crossover probability CR = 0.67. The model become skilled for one hundred generations, with fitness opinions based totally on the accuracy of node class. All experiments have been repeated ten instances, and the common overall performance metrics were recorded to make certain statistical significance. The results were compared towards baseline fashions, including traditional gadget studying algorithms and current GNN frameworks, to demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach.

The overall performance assessment of more than one algorithms, inclusive of Genetic algorithm (GA) (Feng et al. 2024), Particle Swarm Optimization (PSO) (Nagra et al. 2024), gray Wolf Optimizer (GWO) (Hu and Yu 2023), Deep learning (Bhatti et al. 2023), Machine learning (Ileberi and Sun 2024), and the proposed algorithm, was conducted to compare their convergence quotes on a genome dataset. The consequences, as shown in Table 1, spotlight the suggest and exceptional values throughout multiple runs and generations for each set of rules. The convergence overall performance of the proposed algorithm demonstrates a consistent development over traditional techniques like GA, PSO, and GWO. As evident from the growing quantity of generations, the proposed algorithm continues superior convergence, reaching higher fitness costs in both imply and excellent values as compared to other algorithms. This indicates that the hybrid nature of the proposed technique permits for greater powerful exploration and exploitation in the solution space.

Chapter 2 Prediction for Genome Mapping with the Multi-objective Evolutionary Algorithm

Shailendra Pratap Singh

Department of Computer Science and Engineering, Madan Mohan Malaviya University of Technology, Gorakhpur, India.

Email: shail2007singh@gmail.com

DOI: 10.1201/9781003516729-2

Genome mapping plays a crucial role in advancing medical research, evolutionary biology, and personalized medicine. However, processing large and complex genomic data remains a significant challenge. This chapter introduces a novel approach to genome mapping using a multiobjective evolutionary algorithm integrated with the Whale Optimization technique. The proposed method enhances computational efficiency and accuracy through evolutionary optimization, addressing multi-objective challenges such as minimizing computation time while maximizing mapping precision. The algorithm is evaluated using real-world genomic datasets, demonstrating substantial improvements in processing speed and accuracy compared to traditional methods. The results highlight the potential of the proposed framework in accelerating genome analysis and enabling precise predictions in genetic mapping-based datasets.

1. Introduction

Genome mapping is crucial for advancements in medical research, evolutionary biology, and personalized medicine. However, the increasing complexity of genomic data presents challenges in efficient processing and accurate analysis. Traditional genome mapping methods struggle to balance computational efficiency, accuracy, and scalability. Evolutionary algorithms (EAs), inspired by natural evolution, offer effective optimization by leveraging selection, crossover, mutation, and reproduction to refine solutions over generations. Their adaptability makes them well-suited for genome mapping, reducing computation time while maintaining or improving gene sequence precision.

Optimization algorithms have been refined to improve genome mapping efficiency and accuracy. Evolutionary algorithms, such as Genetic Algorithms (Holland 1992), effectively explore large solution spaces, providing nearoptimal results. Researchers have successfully applied these methods to genome assembly and mapping (Zhang and Liu 2000). However, evolutionary algorithms often face high computational demands when handling complex genomic datasets. Techniques like Particle Swarm Optimization (Kennedy and Eberhart 1995) and Simulated Annealing (Kirkpatrick et al. 1983) have improved computational performance but still struggle with accuracy and scalability, especially for large-scale genomic data. Genome mapping remains challenging due to the sheer volume and complexity of genomic information. Traditional methods frequently fall short in speed and precision, particularly with high-throughput sequencing. While optimization strategies—especially those based on evolutionary algorithms—show promise, they require significant computational resources and may struggle to achieve precise mappings within a reasonable timeframe.

This chapter introduces a hybrid approach that integrates whale adaptation learning with a multi-objective evolutionary algorithm to improve genome mapping. By optimizing conflicting objectives like accuracy and efficiency, the framework ensures precise predictions without compromising speed. Evaluated on real-world genomic datasets, the proposed method demonstrates superior performance over traditional approaches, significantly enhancing processing speed, accuracy, and scalability. This hybrid technique presents a promising path for advancing genome analysis and genetic mapping.

1.1 Problem Statement

Genome mapping is a complex computational challenge that involves determining the precise positions of genes and functional elements within a genome. Several obstacles hinder efficient mapping, especially with largescale genomic data. Key limitations of traditional genome mapping methods include:

  • Exponential growth of genomic datasets, making data management difficult.
  • Trade-offs between mapping accuracy and computational efficiency, complicating real-time analysis.
  • Conflicting objectives, such as minimizing computational overhead while maximizing precision, which traditional algorithms struggle to balance.
  • The need for advanced feature extraction, which conventional optimization techniques fail to handle effectively.

To overcome these challenges, this research introduces a hybrid approach combining deep learning with a multi-objective evolutionary algorithm. The optimized genome mapping framework aims to:

  • Minimize computational time for large-scale genomic data.
  • Enhance gene sequence identification and alignment accuracy.
  • Simultaneously optimize multiple conflicting objectives using evolutionary algorithms.

The proposed framework leverages evolutionary algorithms, integrating mutation, crossover, and selection to solve multi-objective challenges. Deep neural networks enhance feature extraction, ensuring accurate genomic sequence representation. This approach addresses conventional bottlenecks, accelerating genome analysis for precise predictions and real-time applications in genetic research.

The chapter is structured as follows: Section 2.2 reviews related work, Section 2.4 details the proposed algorithm, Section 2.5 presents the experimental setup and results, and Section 2.6 concludes with future research directions.

2. Literature Survey

Genome mapping is a vital aspect of genomics, enabling researchers to pinpoint gene locations and understand their functions. Multi-objective optimization (MOO) techniques have increasingly been used to enhance mapping accuracy and efficiency. Early work by (Zhang and Liu 2000) demonstrated the potential of evolutionary algorithms for mapping, integrating genetic algorithms with genomic data to improve accuracy. (Gupta and Jain 2019) advanced this approach by combining deep learning with genetic algorithms for genome-wide association studies, balancing accuracy and computational efficiency. (Schrider and Kern 2018) explored supervised machine learning methods leveraging population genetics, showing how MOO frameworks optimize gene variant identification. (Zhou and Troyanskaya 2015) applied multi-objective optimization to deep learning models, improving prediction accuracy while considering computational cost. Graph neural networks (GNNs) have further influenced genome mapping, with (Angermueller et al. 2016) demonstrating their ability to model complex genomic structures and optimize multiple criteria simultaneously. The continued integration of MOO strategies has significantly advanced genome mapping, helping researchers tackle challenges related to accuracy, efficiency, and computational complexity.

In bioinformatics, Karim et al. (2021) highlight deep learning-based clustering methods, proving effective for gene expression analysis. Kang et al. (2022) develop a hybrid optimization algorithm to enhance feature selection, reinforcing its relevance in bioinformatics. Genome-scale metabolic network models also benefit from intelligent optimization. Bai et al. (2023) explore machine learning-based optimization for metabolic networks, improving computational biology and biomanufacturing applications. Singh et al. (2024) introduce a mutation-based evolutionary algorithm for genomics, demonstrating superior gene selection performance. Zhou and Troyanskaya (2015) showcase deep learning models for predicting noncoding genetic variant outcomes.

Optimization algorithms have significantly advanced healthcare applications. Pradhan et al. (2023) developed a self-adaptive sea lion optimization (SA-SLnO) algorithm for lung cancer detection using deep learning. Joshi and Aziz (2024) proposed a metaheuristic optimization framework integrating gene expression data for brain tumor classification. Reinforcement learning has emerged as a powerful tool for optimization. Hu and Yu (2023) introduced a reinforcement learning-based grey wolf optimizer for feature selection, while Wang et al. (2023) applied reinforcement learning-based particle swarm optimization to complex multi-objective problems. Genetic algorithms (GA) have been effectively used for hyperparameter optimization in deep learning models. Lee et al. (2021) combined GA with deep neural networks to enhance performance. Emam et al. (2024) developed a hybrid optimization framework for cancer diagnosis, utilizing genetic operators for tumor identification. Swarm intelligence has contributed new strategies for feature selection and optimization. Mandle et al. (2024) proposed the Whale Social Spider Optimization algorithm (WSSOA) for brain tumor classification. Gülmez (2023) integrated Grey Wolf Optimization (GWO) with deep learning for cotton disease detection. Several studies have explored optimization algorithms integrated with machine learning to solve complex problems. Karim et al. (2021) highlighted deep learning-based clustering methods for bioinformatics, while Bai et al. (2023) reviewed machine learning and optimization techniques applied to genome-scale metabolic networks.

The literature underscores the growing influence of intelligent optimization algorithms, GNNs, and hybrid deep learning models in bioinformatics, healthcare, and engineering. This chapter introduces a deep learning-based optimization algorithm tailored for genome mapping, integrating neural networks with evolutionary strategies to reduce computational complexity while ensuring high mapping accuracy.

3. Proposed Framework for Genome Mapping-based Prediction

The proposed genome mapping framework integrates multi-objective evolutionary algorithms with genomic techniques to improve accuracy and computational efficiency. It consists of key components designed to optimize mapping precision while minimizing processing time.

3.1 Data Preprocessing

Genomic data undergoes preprocessing to remove noise and irrelevant features. Let D represent genomic sequences and F denote extracted features. The transformation is mathematically expressed as:

 (1)
F′ = P(D)

where P is the preprocessing function that refines raw genomic data into a structured feature set F′.

3.2 Multi-objective Optimization Model

The optimization model aims to maximize the accuracy A of the mapping and minimize the computational time T . The multi-objective function can be expressed as:

 (2)
$${Minimize}\mathbf{F}(x) = \begin{bmatrix} {f_{1}(x)} \\ {f_{2}(x)} \\ \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} {- A(x)} \\ {T(x)} \\ \end{bmatrix}$$

where x is the decision variable representing the parameters of the mapping algorithm. The objectives f1 and f2 are to be minimized concurrently, resulting in a Pareto optimal solution.

3.3 Proposed Fitness Function

To assess genome mapping performance, the fitness function F(x) balances accuracy and computational efficiency:

 (3)
$$F(x) = w_{1} \cdot A(x) + w_{2} \cdot \left( \frac{1}{T(x)} \right)$$

where w1 and w2 are weights, w1 + w2 = 1. A(x) represents mapping accuracy and T (x) denotes computational time.

This function optimizes accuracy while minimizing processing time, for a balanced evaluation.

3.4 Convergence Criteria

The algorithm runs until convergence, determined by the improvement in the Pareto front:

 (4)
$$\Delta = \frac{\left\| F^{t} - F^{t - 1} \right\|}{\left\| F^{t - 1} \right\|} < \varepsilon$$

where Ft and F −1 are the objective values at iterations t and t − 1 and ε is a predefined threshold.

3.5 Output

The proposed framework outputs a set of optimal gene mapping sequences:

 (5)
S* = {s1,s2,…,sk}

where S* represents the optimal sequences, and k is the number of identified sequences. By integrating multi-objective evolutionary algorithms, the framework enhances genome mapping, balancing accuracy and computational efficiency.

4. Proposed Multi-objective Whale Evolutionary Algorithm (MOWEA)

The Multi-objective Whale Evolutionary Algorithm (MOWEA) draws inspiration from humpback whales’ social behavior and hunting strategies. It optimizes multi-objective problems by balancing competing objectives while ensuring computational efficiency. The algorithm follows key steps for effective implementation.

4.1 Algorithm Initialization

The Multi-Objective Whale Algorithm (Sahoo et al. 2024) begins by initializing a population of whales as solutions. Let N be the population size and D the solution space dimensionality. The initial positions are defined as:

 (6)
Xi0 = rand(D), ∀i ∈ [1, N]

where Xi0 represents the position of the i-th whale at iteration t = 0.

4.2 Fitness Evaluation

Each whale’s fitness is assessed using multi-objective functions:

 (7)
Fj(Xi) = fj(Xi), ∀j ∈ [1, M]

where Fj is the j-th objective function, M is the total number of objectives, and fj(Xi) evaluates the j-th objective for i.

4.3 Social Behavior

Whales in the MOWEA utilize two primary behaviors: first exploration, and second exploitation. The exploration phase involves searching for prey, while the exploitation phase involves the use of social interactions to improve solution quality. The position update for a whale can be mathematically expressed as:

 (8)
Xit + 1 = Xbest − A ⋅ |C⋅Xbest−Xit|

where:

-Xbest is the position of the best whale (solution) in the current iteration, - A is a coefficient that controls the exploration ability, calculated as:

 (9)
A = 2a ⋅ r1 − a

  • a decreases linearly from 2 to 0 over the course of iterations,
  • r1 is a random number in the range [0, 1],
  • C is a coefficient that influences the step size, calculated as:

 (10)
C = 2 ⋅ r2

where r2 is another random number in the range [0, 1].

4.4 Fitness Function and Pareto Dominance

To select the best solutions, MOWEA employs Pareto dominance. A solution Xi is said to dominate Xj if:

 (11)
∀k ∈ [1, M], Fk(Xi) ≤ Fk(Xj) ∧ ∃m ∈ [1, M] : Fm(Xi) < Fm(Xj)

The fitness function can be defined as:

 (12)
$$F(X) = \sum\limits_{j = 1}^{M}w_{j} \cdot F_{j}(X)$$

where Δwj is the weight assigned to the j-th objective.

4.5 Termination Criteria

The algorithm iterates until a termination condition is met, which could be based on:

  1. A maximum number of iterations Tmax.
  2. A convergence threshold ε:

 (13)
ΔF < ε

where Δ∆F is the improvement in fitness over iterations. The steps of the proposed MOWEA 1 can be summarized in the following pseudocode:

Algorithm 1 Multi-objective Whale Evolutionary Algorithm (MOWEA)
  • 1: Initialize population Xi0 for i = 1, . . . , N with random positions in the search space
  • 2: for each iteration t = 1 to Tmax do
  • 3: for each whale i do
  • 4: Evaluate fitness Fj(Xi) for all objectives j where j ∈ [1, M]
  • 5: end for
  • 6: Identify Xbest as the best solution based on Pareto dominance
  • 7: for each whale i do

  • 8: Update A using:


    A = 2a ⋅ r1 − a

  • 9: Update C using:


    C = 2 ⋅ r2

  • 10: Update position Xit+1 using:


    Xit + 1 = Xbest − A ⋅ |C⋅Xbest−Xit|

  • 11: end for
  • 12: if termination criteria met, i.e., ∆F < ε or maximum iterations reached then
  • 13: break
  • 14: end if
  • 15: end for
  • 16: return Pareto front of optimal solutions

The MOWEA 2 algorithm integrates exploration and exploitation strategies to optimize multi-objective genome mapping. It simultaneously refines gene sequence predictions while balancing competing objectives. The process begins with a randomly initialized whale population X i 0 for i = 1, . . . , N within the search space. During each iteration t (up to T max ), whales evaluate their fitness F j (X i ) across multiple objectives j, ensuring solution quality. Genome mapping accuracy A(X i ) is computed using predefined criteriaTable 1 and MOWEA Algorithm 2.

Table 1: Terminologies, notations, and descriptions used in MOWEA.⏎
Notation Terminology Description
Xit Position of whale Position of whale i at iteration t in the search space.
N Population size Number of whales in the population.
Tmax Maximum iterations Maximum number of iterations allowed for the algorithm.
Fj(Xi) Fitness function Objective value j for whale i used to assess solutions.
M Number of objectives Total number of objectives to be optimized (multi-objective).
A(Xi) Genome mapping accuracy Accuracy of genome mapping for solution Xi using true positives (TP) and false negatives (FN).
TP True positives Number of correctly predicted gene sequences.
FN False negatives Number of missed gene sequences in prediction.
Xbest Best solution Best solution based on Pareto dominance at the current iteration.
a Control parameter Linearly decreasing parameter that balances exploration and exploitation.
r1, r2 Random numbers Random values in the range [0,1].
A Encircling coefficient Encircling behavior control, updated using a and r1.
C Attraction coefficient Controls attraction between whales, updated using r2.
P(Xi) Genome mapping prediction Prediction result for whale i after optimization.
f (Xi) Prediction function Function mapping input features to predicted gene sequences.
εi Error term Small error added to the prediction to account for uncertainty.
∆F Fitness change Change in fitness value used as a termination criterion.
ε Convergence threshold Small positive value used to determine algorithm convergence.
Algorithm 2 MOWEA Algorithm for Genome Mapping Prediction
  1. 1: Initialize population Xi0fori = 1, …, N with random positions in the search space
  2. 2: for each iteration t = 1 to T max do
  3. 3: for each whale i do
  4. 4: Evaluate fitness Fj (Xi ) for all objectives j where j ∈ [1, M]

  5.  5: Compute genome mapping accuracy A(Xi ) using:


    $$A\left( X_{i} \right) = \frac{TP}{TP + FN}$$

    where TP is the number of true positives and FN is the number of false negatives.

  6. 6: end for
  7. 7: Identify Xbest as the best solution based on Pareto dominance
  8. 8: for each whale i do

  9. 9: Update A using:


    A = 2a ⋅ r1 − a

  10. 10: Update C using:


    C = 2 ⋅ r2

  11. 11: Update position Xit + 1 using:


    Xit + 1 = Xbest − A ⋅ |C⋅Xbest−Xit|

  12.  12: Update genome mapping prediction P(Xi) using:


    P(Xi) = f(Xi) + εi

    where f (Xi) is the function mapping the input features to the predicted gene sequences, and εi is a small error term.

  13. 13: end for
  14. 14: if termination criteria met, i.e., ∆F < ε or maximum iterations reached then
  15. 15: break
  16. 16: end if
  17. 17: end for
  18. 18: return Pareto front of optimal solutions and predicted gene sequences


$$A\left( X_{i} \right) = \frac{TP}{TP + FN}$$

where TP represents the true positives, and FN denotes the false negatives. After assessing fitness, the algorithm identifies the best-performing solution X best based on Pareto dominance criteria. Subsequently, for each whale i, the algorithm updates the position using exploration and exploitation strategies, represented mathematically as:


$$A\left( X_{i} \right) = \frac{TP}{TP + FN}$$

where A and B are coefficient vectors controlling the balance between exploration and exploitation. The exploration and exploitation functions are defined based on the distance to the best solution X best , enabling whales to effectively search for optimal gene sequences in the mapping process. The algorithm continues to iterate until a predefined termination condition is met, such as achieving a satisfactory convergence or reaching T max . Finally, the algorithm returns the Pareto front of optimal solutions, representing the best trade-offs among the multiple objectives in genome mapping.

5. Experimental Setup and Results

The experiments utilized an Intel Core i7-11700 processor, 16 GB of RAM, and Windows 10, implemented in Python 3.11. The MOWEA algorithm was tested on real-world genomic datasets to evaluate mapping prediction, fitness function, loss of function, and error rate.

5.1 Experimental Setup

The algorithm was initialized with a population size of N = 150 and a maximum of T max = 600 iterations. Coefficient vectors A ∈ [−1, 1] and B ∈ [0, 2] were dynamically adjusted to balance exploration and exploitation. A mutation rate of µ = 0.1 and a crossover rate ρ = 0.9 enhanced solution diversity. The algorithm was tested against NSGA-II, MOPSO, and MODE in 15 independent runs for comparison.


$$\begin{array}{l} {A = \frac{TP}{TP + FN}} \\ {R_{c} = \frac{1}{T_{\text{final~}}}{\sum\limits_{t = 1}^{T_{\text{final~}}}F}\left( X_{\text{best~}}^{t} \right)} \\ \end{array}$$

5.2 Datasets

To validate the MOWEA framework, various publicly accessible genomic datasets were used, covering gene expression analysis and genome-wide association studies (GWAS). Each dataset represents a distinct domain within genomic research.

  • The complete genome (HG) sequence from the NCBI Genome Database offers chromosome-specific data, serving as a benchmark for genome mapping algorithm performance.
  • The Arabidopsis (Arabi) reference dataset is widely used for plant genome research and gene function analysis.
  • The GWAS dataset identifies associations between genetic variants and phenotypic traits, serving as a benchmark for evaluating the algorithm’s accuracy in mapping complex traits.

The proposed MOWEA algorithm was compared against NSGA-II (Bohrer and Dorn 2024), MOPSO (Rafie and Moradi 2024), and MODE (Yu et al. 2024). Each algorithm was executed 15 times per dataset to ensure statistical robustness, with results averaged over independent runs. The next section presents detailed experimental comparisons between MOWEA and baseline algorithms.

5.3 Result Discussion: Genome Datasets

Table 2 presents the mean and best values across multiple runs and generations for each algorithm. The genome prediction performance was evaluated using four algorithms: NSGA-II (Bohrer and Dorn 2024), MOPSO (Rafie and Moradi 2024), MODE (Yu et al. 2024), and the proposed algorithm. Results indicate that prediction accuracy improves across all algorithms as generations increase. For the HG dataset, NSGA-II’s accuracy rose from 0.3888 at 20 generations to 0.5892 at 360 generations. The proposed algorithm outperformed others, achieving 0.7272 accuracy at 360 generations. The GWAS dataset, being the most complex, showed lower accuracy across all algorithms, while the HG and Arabi datasets demonstrated higher overall accuracy. The proposed algorithm consistently performed best, confirming its scalability and robustness in long-term optimization. Its superior accuracy, especially in complex datasets, validates its effectiveness for genome prediction tasks, making it a promising approach for genome mapping.

The accuracy for the NSGA-II (Bohrer and Dorn 2024), MOPSO (Rafie and Moradi 2024), MODE (Yu et al. 2024), and the proposed algorithm was evaluated over 600 generations across three datasets: HG, Arabi, and GWAS. For the HG dataset, the proposed algorithm consistently outperformed others, reaching 0.94 accuracy, followed by MODE (0.87), MOPSO (0.85), and NSGA-II (0.74). In the Arabi dataset, the proposed algorithm again achieved the highest accuracy (0.92), with MODE (0.89), MOPSO (0.83), and NSGA-II (0.80) showing competitive performance. The GWAS dataset followed a similar trend, with the proposed algorithm leading at 0.90 accuracy, MODE at 0.89, MOPSO at 0.88, and NSGA-II at 0.75. These results highlight the superior optimization capability of the proposed algorithm, particularly in complex, high-dimensional genomic environments (Bohrer and Dorn 2024, Rafie and Moradi 2024, Yu et al. 2024).

5.4 Discussion on Genome Predictions

The proposed algorithm consistently achieved the lowest fitness values across all datasets over 600 generations. For the Human Genome dataset, fitness cost decreased from 0.212 to 0.62328, demonstrating steady convergence. In comparison, NSGA-II (Bohrer and Dorn 2024), MOPSO (Rafie and Moradi 2024), MODE (Yu et al. 2024) algorithms recorded fitness values of 0.60204, 0.60987, and 0.64206, respectively, indicating the superior optimization capability of the proposed method. Similar trends were observed in the Arabidopsis and GWAS datasets, where the proposed algorithm consistently maintained an advantage in reducing fitness costs, highlighting its robustness across diverse genomic datasets. MOPSO and MODE exhibited stronger trends, converging at 0.87 and 0.873, respectively. The proposed algorithm achieved the highest fitness, demonstrating superior optimization for genome mapping. In Figure 5, the Arabi dataset showed a steady increase in fitness values. NSGA-II reached 0.85, while MOPSO and MODE: GWAS Accuracy Comparison for Various Algorithms over 600 Gen closely followed at 0.86 and 0.88, respectively. The proposed algorithm again outperformed others, reaching 0.93 at 600 generations, highlighting its robustness in handling complex genome mapping tasks.

5.5 Discussion on Error Rate and Fitness Cost in Genome Datasets

Table 4 compares the fitness cost of genome datasets across generations and runs for NSGA-II (Bohrer and Dorn 2024), MOPSO (Rafie and Moradi 2024), MODE (Yu et al. 2024), and the proposed algorithm. Similarly, in the Arabi and GWAS datasets, the proposed algorithm demonstrated superior convergence rates, reducing error rates significantly after 600 generations. Overall, while NSGA-II, MOPSO, and MODE showed gradual improvements, their convergence was slower compared to the proposed method. The proposed algorithm effectively minimizes errors, showcasing robustness and adaptability across diverse datasets.

For the Arabi dataset (Figure 9), the proposed algorithm achieves faster convergence, reducing fitness cost from 0.972 to 0.53. 5.7 Heatmap Analysis of Cross-Entropy Loss for Genome Prediction Datasets

The performance of four algorithms—NSGA-II (Bohrer and Dorn 2024), MOPSO (Rafie and Moradi 2024), MODE (Yu et al. 2024), and the proposed algorithm—was evaluated across three genome prediction datasets: Human Genome, Arabidopsis, and GWAS. The heatmap in Figure 13 illustrates the cross-entropy loss for each combination. The proposed algorithm consistently outperformed others, achieving the lowest loss values: 0.30 for Human Genome and GWAS, and 0.28 for Arabidopsis. MODE exhibited higher loss values, particularly in Arabidopsis (0.37), while MOPSO and NSGA-II showed similar performance across datasets, with NSGA-II scoring 0.37 for Human Genome. MOPSO maintained stability but had higher losses, indicating room for optimization. The findings highlight the superior optimization capabilities of the proposed algorithm, especially in complex datasets like Arabidopsis. The heatmap confirms its effectiveness in reducing prediction errors compared to other multi-objective optimization methods.

6. Conclusion and Future Work

This chapter presents a comparative analysis of the proposed algorithm for genome prediction across three datasets: Human Genome, Arabidopsis, and GWAS. The performance of four algorithms—NSGA-II (Bohrer and Dorn 2024), MOPSO (Rafie and Moradi 2024), MODE (Yu et al. 2024), and the proposed algorithm—was evaluated. Results indicate that the proposed algorithm significantly outperforms others in accuracy, prediction, and error reduction across all datasets. This highlights the importance of selecting appropriate optimization strategies to enhance predictive accuracy in genome-related datasets. While traditional algorithms like NSGA-II and MOPSO demonstrate stable performance, their loss values remain higher. The superior performance of the proposed algorithm underscores the advantages of hybrid and adaptive optimization techniques for genome mapping and prediction tasks.

6.1 Future Scope

Future research can expand this study by:

  • Evaluating the scalability of the proposed algorithm on larger, more complex genome datasets.
  • Integrating the algorithm with real-time genome analysis systems to enhance personalized medicine applications.
  • Optimizing multiple conflicting objectives, such as prediction accuracy and computational efficiency, to broaden its use.
  • Adapting the optimization framework for proteomics, transcriptomics, and drug discovery, to improve data prediction and analysis.

For insights into genome analysis scalability and optimization, you can explore this study or this review.

  • Gupta, A. and Jain, S. (2019). A hybrid deep learning and genetic algorithm-based approach for genome-wide association studies. Bioinformatics, 35(4): 698–705.
  • Holland, J. H. (1992). Adaptation in Natural and Artificial Systems. MIT Press.
  • Hu, Z. and Yu, X. (2023). Reinforcement learning-based comprehensive learning grey wolf optimizer for feature selection. Applied Soft Computing, 149: 110959.
  • Joshi, A. A. and Aziz, R. M. (2024). Deep learning approach for brain tumor classification using metaheuristic optimization with gene expression data. International Journal of Imaging Systems and Technology, 34(2): e23007.

  • Karim, M. R., Beyan, O., Zappa, A., Costa, I. G., Rebholz-Schuhmann, D., Cochez, M. and Decker, S. (2021). Deep learning-based clustering approaches for bioinformatics. Briefings in Bioinformatics, 22(1): 393–415.
  • Kennedy, J. and Eberhart, R. (1995). Particle swarm optimization. In Proceedings of the IEEE International Conference on Neural Networks, pp. 1942–1948.

  • Lee, S., Kim, J., Kang, H., Kang, D. -Y. and Park, J. (2021). Genetic algorithm based deep learning neural network structure and hyperparameter optimization. Applied Sciences, 11(2): 744.
  • Mandle, A. K., Sahu, S. P. and Gupta, G. P. (2024). WSSOA: Whale Social Spider Optimization Algorithm for brain tumor classification using deep learning technique. International Journal of Information Technology, pp. 1–17.
  • Pradhan, K., Chawla, P. and Rawat, S. (2023). A deep learning-based approach for detection of lung cancer using self adaptive sea lion optimization algorithm (sa-slno). Journal of Ambient Intelligence and Humanized Computing, 14(9): 12933–12947.
  • Rafie, A. and Moradi, P. (2024). A multi-objective gene selection for cancer diagnosis using particle swarm optimization and mutual information. Journal of Ambient Intelligence and Humanized Computing, pp. 1–17.
  • Sahoo, S. K., Premkumar, M., Saha, A. K., Houssein, E. H., Wanjari, S. and Emam, M. M. (2024). Multi-objective quasi-reflection learning and weight strategy-based moth flame optimization algorithm. Neural Computing and Applications, 36(8): 4229–4261.
  • Schrider, D. R. and Kern, A. D. (2018). Supervised machine learning for population genetics: A new paradigm. Trends in Genetics, 34(4): 301–312.
  • Singh, S. P., Yadav, D. K., Chamran, M. K. and Perera, D. G. (2024). Intelligent mutation based evolutionary optimization algorithm for genomics and precision medicine. Functional & Integrative Genomics, 24(4): 128.
  • Wang, M., Li, X., Chen, L. and Chen, H. (2023). Medical machine learning based on multiobjective evolutionary algorithm using learning decomposition. Expert Systems with Applications, 216: 119450.
  • Yu, X., Xu, P., Wang, F. and Wang, X. (2024). Reinforcement learning-based differential evolution algorithm for constrained multi-objective optimization problems. Engineering Applications of Artificial Intelligence, 131: 107817.
  • Zhang, Z. and Liu, J. S. (2000). Genome mapping by evolutionary algorithms. Bioinformatics, 16(12): 1105–1114.
  • Zhou, J. and Troyanskaya, O. G. (2015). Predicting effects of noncoding variants with deep learning-based sequence model. Nature Methods, 12(10): 931–934.

Chapter 3 Deep Learning and Medical Data Engineering for Knowledge Discovery

Madhuri Gupta,1

Emails: madhuri.gupta@bennett.edu.in, deepanshichoudhary1408@gmail.com, prabhisheksingh88@gmail.com, manoj.diwakar@gmail.coma

DOI: 10.1201/9781003516729-3

We are generating vast amounts of data, yet its relevance remains uncertain. Data engineering has traditionally focused on big data-storing and processing large datasets—but the real value lies in transforming data into knowledge. Deep learning bridges this gap by developing algorithmic expertise in specific domains. This research introduces Domain Driven Data Engineering, emphasizing that data engineering processes are shaped by their application domain. We propose a refined roadmap of data engineering phases based on this methodology, enhancing previous frameworks. For further insights, you can explore this roadmap or this methodology.

1. Introduction

Knowledge discovery is the intelligent process of extracting valid, novel and useful patterns from data, extending beyond logical analysis (Yamazaki et al. 2022, Li et al. 2021). It involves data management, cleaning, preprocessing, modeling, and evaluation, alongside usability and knowledge presentation. This chapter explores knowledge discovery techniques across various models, knowledge engineering and technology-driven applications for efficient pattern identification in large datasets. It also examines application engineering, discovery challenges, confirmatory research costs, and connections to machine learning, data visualization, and automated programming.

Machine learning (ML) is evolving rapidly, driven by deeper insights into human neural networks. Once limited to experts, ML now tackles complex problems in image/audio processing, natural language understanding, automation, and autonomous systems (Samek et al. 2021, Pulvermüller et al. 2021). Artificial neural networks excel in solving intricate challenges despite transparency concerns (Yang and Wang 2020, Galván and Mooney 2021). Their success spans diverse applications, from gene clustering to optimizing data engineering processes (Liu et al. 2021, Taye 2023). This research emphasizes knowledge discovery, advocating for pre-aggregation in data engineering to refine patterns and enhance predictive accuracy. Proper data assembly ensures meaningful insights, validating theoretical models for practical use.

1.1 Core Components in the Medical Field

Deep Learning and Medical Data Engineering for Knowledge Discovery integrates AI-driven techniques with healthcare data processing to enhance patient outcomes and clinical decision-making.

  • Deep Learning: Advanced models like CNNs, RNNs, and transformers analyze medical images and patient records, identifying patterns beyond human perception to improve diagnostics, treatment planning and prognosis prediction.
  • Medical Data Engineering: Focuses on efficient data management, including preprocessing, transformation, and feature engineering, ensuring usability while maintaining privacy and security.
  • Knowledge Discovery: Extracts trends, risk factors and causal relationships in disease patterns, informing clinical guidelines and treatment strategies through deep learning pipelines.

Applications in Healthcare:

  • Medical Imaging: Deep learning models enhance CT, MRI, and X-ray segmentation and classification for accurate disease detection.
  • Genomics: Identifies mutations and patterns in genetic data linked to diseases.
  • Electronic Health Records (EHR): Analyzes patient histories, symptoms, and medications, to predict disease progresses and side effects.
  • Predictive Modeling: Forecasts patient outcomes and recommends optimal treatment paths.

This rapidly evolving field is crucial for advancing next-generation healthcare and personalized medicine.

2. Background and Motivation

Over a decade ago, during the era of narrow AI, researchers focused on how humans learn from stimuli like images, voice, and touch (Sejnowski 2020, Sarker 2021). AI advancements raised concerns about automation displacing jobs, with some fearing professional alienation (Xu et al. 2021, Castiglioni et al. 2021). Now in the age of general AI, deep learning has evolved rapidly, shaping policy, industry, and open-source initiatives (Wang et al. 2023, Saxe et al. 2021). This shift emphasizes new architectures, data engineering, and computing strategies, solidifying deep learning theory (Saha et al. 2021, Janiesch et al. 2021, Matsuo et al. 2022, Gupta et al. 2021).

As health and security challenges grow, adopting Knowledge

Discovery in Databases (KDD) within ethical frameworks becomes crucial Solutions to pressing global issues will continue to expand, sparking debates and competition (Hendrycks et al. 2021, Cobbe et al. 2021). Economic, cultural, legal, environmental, social, and political complexities demand multidisciplinaryAI-driven approaches. With increasing data on case types and trends, AI algorithms enhance problem-solving across diverse domains (Djurayeva 2023).

This section explores the evolution of data science, data engineering, and deep learning, covering key technologies, techniques, and algorithms. The survey is divided into six subsections (Yang et al. 2022, Sarker 2021):

  1. Introduction to Data Science.
  2. Detailed discussions on data mining, statistics, machine learning, data engineering, and knowledge discovery.
  3. Big data platforms for data engineering.
  4. Advancements in deep learning (Sharifani and Amini 2023).

In recent decades, the surge in data generated by human activity has accelerated knowledge development (Kaluarachchi et al. 2021, Sharma et al. 2021). Knowledge discovery applications—such as classification, clustering, outlier detection, and association rule mining—support business evaluation, fraud detection, and recommendation systems (Pan et al. 2022, Campesato 2020, Zhang et al. 2020). Data science has emerged as an interdisciplinary field integrating data mining, statistics, data engineering, and machine learning (Yeturu 2020, Sharma et al. 2021, Sarker et al. 2020). Machine learning a subset of AI, extracts knowledge from data and intersects with data science. Its integration with data engineering enhances deep learning capabilities. This work, based on ISiC’s tutorial and related research, explores deep learning concepts, data engineering for knowledge discovery and model interpretability (Pramod et al. 2021, Mukhamediev et al. 2022).

2.1 Foundations of Deep Learning

Deep learning has transformed AI research excelling in object recognition, semantic detection, NLP, machine translation, and autonomous driving. By leveraging vast data, and advanced algorithms, it has surpassed traditional methods across industrial and scientific domains, becoming a key approach in business applications. Key aspects in knowledge discovery include:

  1. Multi-Layered Perceptron (MLP): Since the 1980s, backpropagation enabled training of deep networks, forming the foundation for modern deep learning (Worden et al. 2023). Convolutional and recurrent networks are specialized versions of MLP, balancing simplicity and depth (Ünal and Başçiftçi 2022).
  2. Activation Functions: These enable non-linear combinations of projections, allowing deep networks to model complex decision boundaries beyond linear approximations.

Several Deep Learning Techniques are described as follows:

A. Neural Networks

Artificial neural networks (ANNs) are statistical models inspired by the neural architecture of biological brains (Rosoł et al. 2022). While biological and artificial neurons differ fundamentally, both function as elementary units that receive, process, and transmit signals—key components in reasoning and perception.

The connectionist perspective challenges logical representations of knowledge (Xia et al. 2021). Artificial neural networks address the mind-body problem by establishing causal links between units, enabling both information exchange and intelligence (Tank et al. 2021). Despite their success in solving complex problems, the debate over connectionist vs. logical-symbolic AI—regarding efficiency and ability to emulate human intelligence—remains unresolved.

B. Transfer Learning

Deep neural networks (DNNs) retain task-agnostic features like edges and object patterns, making them valuable for various applications (Neyshabur et al. 2020). Transfer learning leverages pretrained DNNs trained on large datasets, adapting them to new tasks by reusing domain knowledge. This process can involve training a new output layer or fine-tuning the full network. The pretrained network serves as the foundation for efficient learning (Chakraborty et al. 2022, Jiang et al. 2022).

Transfer learning is valuable when labeled data is scarce, as deep neural networks (DNNs) require large datasets for effective generalization (Iman et al. 2023, Ullah et al. 2022). By leveraging pretrained models, it enhances performance across various applications. While highly successful in image-related tasks, it also benefits text, video, and conversation-based datasets (Brodzicki et al. 2020).

C. Deep Learning Architectures

Deep learning architectures transform data representations across hierarchical layers (Bachute and Subhedar 2021). These include deep neural networks, deep belief networks, and recurrent neural networks evolving with hardware advancements, data availability, and increasing model complexity (Aslan and Yilmaz 2021, Sengupta et al. 2020). Deep learning enhances abstraction levels in extracted knowledge (Ezzat et al. 2021). A two-layer perceptron combined with restricted Boltzmann machines enables hierarchical feature learning, strengthening deep architectures.

Deep architectures emulate functions through layered learning, increasing feature complexity while simplifying training (Khan et al. 2020). Their effectiveness stems from optimizing criteria that require deeper hierarchies when shallow methods fall short (Rashid et al. 2020, Chen et al. 2020). Layering advancements have significantly improved sensor and image processing, with feedback mechanisms enhancing recognition accuracy. Soft recognition plays a crucial role in handling noisy speech data enabling deep architectures to extract complex structures for advanced image analysis.

2.2 Reinforcement Learning

Reinforcement learning (RL), enables an agent to learn an optimal behavior strategy (policy) to maximize rewards. Model-free RL skips estimating system dynamics and reward models, relying on direct interactions for learning (Wang et al. 2022, Colas et al. 2022). The agent refines its policy through repeated execution (Yang and Wang 2020).

Reinforcement learning relies on feedback—typically a numerical reward, but sometimes binary (success/failure) or categorical (ranking) indicators—to refine an agent’s policy. Solving problems with RL requires defining simple tasks that guide the agent toward optimal solutions (Zhang et al. 2021).

In each trial, the RL agent observes the system’s state, selects an action based on its policy, and requests execution (Zai and Brown 2020, Liu et al. 2021). After execution, the agent evaluates the new state and received reward, updating its policy accordingly (Wang et al. 2022). To assess action effectiveness, total return summarizes cumulative rewards over time. In simple RL tasks, it depends solely on rewards obtained at each step (Hambly et al. 2023, Liu et al. 2020).

2.3 Data Engineering

Data engineering is essential for data science, ensuring the right data is available for analysis (Yang et al. 2022). This chapter outlines key skills for deep learning and future trends (Alzubaidi et al. 2023). Historically, data science faced challenges due to limited data and a lack of big data tools, but advancements are transforming the field (Amanullah et al. 2020). As data science tools integrated with big data platforms, large-scale operations became central to data engineering (Giorgi et al. 2022). Instead of designing specialized skills, Hadoop tools were introduced to data scientists, leading to inefficient resource use (Zhang et al. 2020). Data engineering responsibilities shifted to data scientists, often through trial-and-error, absorbing tasks that don’t require deep expertise (Raschka et al. 2020). Meanwhile, critical skills like data governance and ETL remained separate to ensure proper data control.

A. Data Collection and Preparation

In knowledge discovery, data is the foundation (Munappy et al. 2022). The data engineering ecosystem addresses data collection, encoding, cleaning, annotation, and presentation for mining (Kotsiopoulos et al. 2021). Deep learning enables a streamlined data engineering approach, efficiently supporting diverse knowledge paradigms. We propose a wired-layer practice, prioritizing data-centric neural processing for optimal results (Soni and Kumar 2022).

Data engineering, a key data preparation and processing domain, has evolved with the Internet to support web data and API outputs (Wang et al. 2020, Khder 2021). Modern cloud architectures enhance usability with eventtriggered interfaces, streamlining data ingestion and transformation. While data pipelines facilitate structured processing, advanced mechanisms like Expando are needed for data scraping, recombination, and rewriting when handling unstructured data.

Data engineering focuses on data flow, utilizing tuples (vectorial units) in deep learning (Yang et al. 2022, Yin et al. 2020). In machine learning and data mining, entities vary—ranging from unstructured text blobs, 2D patterns, tree-like structures, and nested formats (Sriram et al. 2020, Kreuzberger et al. 2023). These elements integrate into models through type detection ensuring schema consistency, immutability, or non-destructive preprocessing.

2.4 Data Storage and Management

Big data storage is essential for handling vast datasets efficiently (Janssen et al. 2020). A comprehensive strategy ensures cost-effective, fast, and versatile storage solutions. Data management involves organizing and optimizing storage technologies while maintaining security, governance, compliance, and performance (Zaabar et al. 2021, Yaqoob et al. 2022).

Traditional storage relied on hard drives and SSDs, limiting databases to 300TB–400TB (Kausar and Nasar 2021, Hassan 2021). As data demands grew, NoSQL emerged to handle unstructured data, with cloud storage becoming the future (Malik et al. 2020). Big data requires high parallelism, leading to Massive Parallel Processing (MPP) for efficient data warehousing (Pansara 2020). Secure, scalable storage solutions like data warehouses and data marts optimize retrieval, reporting, and analytics.

Data should not be managed in silos—a robust data management system is essential for big data and knowledge discovery (Diène et al. 2020). As data proliferates, companies face growing challenges. During ERP transitions, organizations often discard older data, but secure auditing and organization are crucial for maintaining valuable insights.

2.5 Feature Engineering

Feature engineering in data science involves elaboration and selection of relevant features for predictive models (Sarkar and De Bruyn 2021). Features must convey meaningful information for the predictive task (Horn et al. 2020). This process is crucial for large datasets or heterogeneous data sources, requiring iterative refinement beyond pre-processing.

2.6 Knowledge Graphs: Structure and Applications

Knowledge graphs integrate multiple data sources through shared concepts (vertices) using key-value pairs to link decision variables via predicates (Ji et al. 2021, Ryen et al. 2022). Their strength lies in explicit interconnections, simplifying model construction and execution. While blockchains and GraphQL enable similar relationships via transactions and API specifications, they maintain distinct data sources. Unlike knowledge graphs, these systems rely on post-merge interpretation rather than direct predicate application (Weikum 2021).

Researchers can weld data sources by applying regularization techniques with a soft constraint covariance term for each dataset (Arat 2022). Crossvalidation helps determine covariance for singleton dimensions in the prediction matrix Y(t), while cosine similarity is used to de-correlate pairwise constraints (Cerda and Varoquaux 2020). This approach connects low-cardinality decision processes with high-cardinality knowledge base inference through jointly shaped embeddings.

2.7 Model Training and Evaluation

This phase covers the entire CRISP-DM modeling process, including sample extraction, model development mechanisms, and refinement strategies (Saltz 2021, Firas 2023). A key focus is determining model robustness and validity. In big data contexts, optimization often involves parallel processing with Apache Spark, semaphores for iterative adaptation, and Apache Mesos/YARN for distributed job management and cluster optimization (Kannengiesser and Gero 2023).

Model development starts with defining the target attribute, the variables used for predictions (Plotnikova et al. 2021). Data partitioning helps assess model performance, with the number of partitions influencing refining operations (Darwich et al. 2021, Mitchell et al. 2021). Training involves sample data collection, creating training sets for validation. In big data, test sets may be large enough to allow forward selection techniques for feature selection (Wasalathanthri et al. 2020).

2.8 Ethical Challenges in Deep Learning and Big Data

While deep learning and big data offer transformative potential, they also pose ethical concerns and privacy risks. This chapter explores key issues, including:

  • Ethical Considerations in Data Engineering.
  • Data engineering and deep learning raise privacy concerns and ethical challenges (Lu et al. 2021, Marzari et al. 2021).

Deep learning extracts insights by processing low-level data from subtasks, while big data offers solutions but also introduces new vulnerabilities (Lu et al. 2021).

Ethical concerns in big data analytics are increasingly urgent. Many professions already follow domain-specific ethical guidelines, often enforced by certification boards (Gou et al. 2023). In healthcare, non-expert judgment aligns with the medical standard of care, emphasizing a commitment to do no harm. This commercial preemption through professional regulations shapes the broader ethical landscape of big data.

3. Artificial Intelligence

3.1 Natural Language Processing

Social media and the internet provide diverse public opinions, essential for policy and commercial decisions (Han et al. 2020). However, natural language data often contains noise and bias, making consistent extraction difficult. Rapidly evolving concepts and lexicons further complicate analysis, as traditional domain-specific lexicons become outdated (Melton et al. 2021, Dubois et al. 2020). To address this, we need specialized lexicons and advanced tools for detecting and extracting insights from open-source data, including speech, news and social media.

Natural language processing (NLP) tackles diverse language challenges using AI tools (Fanni et al. 2023, Lauriola et al. 2022). Data mining researchers extract structured knowledge from multilingual sources for tasks like clustering and classification (Khurana et al. 2023, Mah et al. 2022). NLP enables task-specific models, including predictive sentiment analysis and named entity recognition, while also refining domain-specific lexicons for evolving datasets.

3.2 Computer Vision

Computer vision automates ophthalmic image analysis, detecting and classifying retinal diseases, macular edema, and neovascularization based on image characteristics (Mistry and Ramakrishnan 2023, Nazir et al. 2020). Deep learning enhances medical image analysis, focusing on diabetic retinal edema, age-related macular degeneration, glaucoma, and retinal neovascularization (Sarki et al. 2020, Karthiyayini and Shenbagavadivu 2021). Techniques include deep belief networks, wavelet transformation, K-means clustering, and histogram matching, with support vector machine (SVM) performance comparisons (Badar et al. 2020).

Occlusion changes through histogram matching outperform K-means sparsity and wavelet high-frequency features when applied SVM classification (Behzadi-Khormouji and Rostami 2021, Uchiyama et al. 2023). Across datasets, occlusion changes surpass super-pixel CNNs suggesting deep features and kernel-based classifiers don’t require complex architectures with large datasets. Additionally, rigid body movement of the fundus camera, lens, and retina was analyzed during retinal imaging (Wang et al. 2021).

3.3 Time Series Analysis

Time series analysis originates from observing natural phenomena to correlate and forecast events (Schaffer et al. 2021, Hugonnet et al. 2021). Economic and financial time series gained attention as models evolved from simple to complex. Despite advancements, many aspects remain theoretically unexplained, as prior domain knowledge constrains strict relationships. Existing physical laws limit possible models, while formalizations grow with the challenge of defining deterministic models (Santoro et al. 2023).

Time series analysis primarily relies on statistical models, except for deterministic approaches (Schaffer et al. 2021). Statistical methods are used when theoretical relationships between parameters are unclear (Hamilton 2020), while deterministic models estimate relationships directly from the data. Machine learning introduces flexible models, such as neural networks, enabling parameter estimation through data cleaning and feature extraction (Tavenard et al. 2020).

4. Applications of Deep Learning and Data Engineering in Knowledge Discovery

Deep learning and data engineering tackle complex challenges, helping domain experts assess their full potential, economic impact and human cost (Yang et al. 2022, Dargan et al. 2020).

Unsupervised deep learning aids domain experts in developing novel theories, with convolutional visualization enhancing model interpretation (Zhang et al. 2022). Protein design benefits from deep learning, predicting mutations to improve protein sequences efficiently (Sarker 2021). Regression learning predicts complex experimental outcomes, addressing challenges in non-simulated environments (Li et al. 2022). Natural language processing (NLP) applies CNNs for sentiment analysis, integrating Twitter and news data for insights.

4.1 Anomaly Detection

Anomaly detection, or outlier/novelty detection, identifies weaknesses in security, fraud detection, and rare behaviors (Pang et al. 2021, Aldweesh et al. 2020). Traditional methods relied on shallow learning, using one-class classification or semi-supervised learning to address the scarcity of labeled anomalies. Recent advancements introduced Deep Information Preserving Generative Adversarial Networks, which create hierarchical factorizations to separate object and scene properties, enhancing anomaly detection capabilities (Ruff et al. 2021).

In classical statistics, outliers are data points that deviate significantly from the majority, while robust statistics define them as unlikely to originate from the same process during exploratory data analysis (EDA) (BlázquezGarcía et al. 2021, Lai et al. 2021). Traditional univariate methods normalize data and flag extreme values, but in multi-dimensional spaces, absolute differences become unreliable due to the curse of dimensionality (Duraj and Szczepaniak 2021). To address this, Earth-mover distance is used for time series analysis, treating each series as a distribution (Balakrishnan and Sangiambut 2021).

5. Challenges and Future Directions

Data collection is a time-intensive and complex step in the knowledge discovery often becoming a bottleneck in research (Rotondo and Quilligan 2020). As datasets grow, collecting data for machine learning algorithms in big data applications becomes increasingly difficult (Ghazal and Hammad 2022, Taherdoost 2021). Surveys and questionnaires are key tools in social and environmental science, but effective design requires expertise in survey methodology and pedagogy (Schneider and Harknett 2022). Survey datasets often suffer from bias, impacting data reliability (Lehdonvirta et al. 2021). Survey questions often require interpretation and assumptions, leading to measurement errors that complicate modeling accuracy (Eyal et al. 2021). Poor survey design results in low-quality data, as respondents may misinterpret, answer incorrectly, or skip unclear questions (Pradhan et al. 2023). Managing missing data often requires unrealistic assumptions, complicating analysis (Emmanuel et al. 2021, Jäger et al. 2021). Surveys face inefficiencies when using multiple open-ended questions, making qualitative data collection timeconsuming.

Automatic question generation offers a solution, optimizing survey design and improving data collection efficiency.

6. Conclusion

This chapter explores data engineering and deep neural networks for handling high-volume, high-dimensional data, ensuring accuracy, representation generation, and computational efficiency. The findings extend beyond model design, offering insights for data-intensive applications. Deep learning and data engineering effectively tackle complex challenges across finance, transportation, healthcare, agriculture, climate, communication, marketing, urban ecology, and mathematical modeling.

Our approach emphasizes label-generation algorithms, where deep neural networks rely on automatically identified features from input data. These features form n-dimensional structures, eliminating the need for a physical model explanation. Testing and validation confirm efficiency, particularly in handling feature space and noise throughout the learning process. The input undergoes data abstraction before reaching the classifier, ensuring durability and accuracy.
